Point Report by the Committee on Foreign Affairs on Amendment No 2315/A(E) by Mrs Angela Lueger, Mr Werner Amon, Mr MBA, colleagues on the dramatic situation in Syria and its impact on the region and also on Europe (2422 D.B.)
ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e8291	We'll get to the 1st. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. We are therefore going into the debate. Mr Amon is the first to speak. - Please, you're talking.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e8321	Madam President. Mr. State Secretary! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Today, in this foreign policy debate, we are dealing, among other things, with the situation in Syria and the dramatic humanitarian situation experienced by people who have to live in unimaginably bad conditions. It's probably true that the scenario that's playing there is massively tangling Europe. There are waves of refugees to Jordan, to Turkey, to Lebanon, and we are experiencing a humanitarian disaster in which the community of states is massively called for. But we also experience – and that is, I believe, something that has to do with us in a special way – once again a massive persecution of Christians in Syria. There are about 2 million Christians in Syria, about 10 percent of the population now affected by massive expulsion, robbery, targeted killings, mass rapes. This may have become a little more aware of the abduction of the two Metropolitans of Aleppo. As a result, the overall situation is perhaps a little stronger in the face of the public. The situation in Syria is completely unclear. There are different groups that want to overthrow the Assad regime, and there is actually no state in the classical sense, which is, so to speak, still master of the situation, even with regard to any international agreements. Of course, this tangles Austria in so far as we have been stationing troops on the Golan Heights for 40 years in the United Nations. The historic conflict opponents of Israel and Syria agreed with a so-called Disengagement Force, a United Nations force that has a kind of neutral area to deal with these former historical opponents. This is an observer mission, a reporting mission, not a robust United Nations mission with heavy arms. On the contrary, the troops at the Golan Heights are only equipped with light weapons, which fulfils the task and mandate of the United Nations. Due to the massive fighting in Syria, which is currently being "guarded" by the UN forces, if one wants to do so, there has now been a situation assessment in the defence resort. In this situation assessment, the Minister of State for Foreign Affairs recently proposed to the Council of Ministers that the Austrian troops withdraw from the Golan Heights of this mission. This, ladies and gentlemen, is a far-reaching decision, which, I believe, can be viewed as quite differentiated insofar as the situation is, of course, to be taken seriously. If the defence resort reports that one can no longer control the situation, then the decision must be made to pull the troops off the Golan Heights. This has been done by the Federal Government because the security of our soldiers must be our top priority, ladies and gentlemen. If we are pursuing reporting in the international media, we can well see that it has grown to form it positively. [[Abg. Petzner: Haha, very positively formulated!]] There are plenty of opinions. In the Arab area, for example, Al Jazeera – and that is not insignificant – reports quite objectively about this decision. We find a dramatic criticism in Jewish media, although I, I would say, must reject relevant stereotypes that are inappropriate. The “International Herald Tribune” for example – and this probably brings the diversity of the matter to the fore – wrote two days ago: “You can’t really blame the Austrian government for not wanting to risk the lives of its troops. On the other hand, what's the point of having international forces at all if they are pulled out when things get tough?" So with one word: On the one hand, the Austrian Government cannot be accused of withdrawing its troops if the situation is the way it is and the current mandate of the United Nations is not enough. On the other hand, the question of what international troops might be for when they are deducted when it becomes so to speak difficult. In this context, however, I welcome the Minister for Defence in our midst, it is precisely the issue of the international community of states, because, I believe, it would have already been appropriate – in particular from the Permanent Members of the UN Security Council – to think in time about the question of this mandate, because the mandate in the current form is not sufficient to carry out a continuation of this mission. So there are two dimensions in this discussion, and I think you have to look at both. One dimension is the assessment of the military situation. This has been carried out by the Minister of Defense with its experts in the Defence Division and then informed the Federal Government that a continuation of the mission after the situation assessment and on the basis of the mandate of the United Nations is no longer to be carried out. That is why the Federal Government has made the decision to bring our troops home. The second dimension is the external policy, which must not be underestimated, namely: How is the UN going to deal with such situations in the future? However, it is less the UN in discussion, especially the Permanent Members of the United Nations Security Council who, in my opinion, already have to consider how to deal with it in the future. Ladies and gentlemen, there was still a debate, and I believe that the Foreign Minister has done just that by calling for the maintenance of the arms embargo at the level of the European Union. – Absolutely due to a completely inconspicuous situation in Syria: on the one hand, troops of the state of Syria, on the other hand, an inconspicuous arrangement of – under quotations – “rebels”. Both sides have been guilty of war crimes according to reports by both Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International. War crimes take place on both sides. In such a situation, in order to provide any side in addition to arms, only our rejection can be found, ladies and gentlemen. That is, I think, the case incontrovertibly. It is unfortunate that the European Union did not find a common procedure on this issue, but at least there was no decision on our part to support such a wrong procedure, ladies and gentlemen. And then, of course, this is also the question: How do you deal with getting our soldiers home? I think this is neither a cause for triumph nor a special joy. It is a very, very serious matter if such a decision is to be made with all international implications. So I mean, the photo shoot that happened yesterday at the airport might have been well meant, but sometimes well meant the opposite of good, ladies and gentlemen.

It is important to prevent the military spark from going over to Lebanon, Israel or other neighbouring countries. We too bear responsibility here, both humanitarian and political. In conclusion, I also call on the Federal Government to do everything in its power to strengthen political and diplomatic pressure so that we do not finally have to continue to see how civilians and innocent people in Syria must die. - Thank you many times.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e11323	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! This centre is, of course, controversial if you follow the opinions here in the National Council, the Internet forums and the like. Finally, we too have decided to be against this centre because we say that the name of an absoluteist, autocratic ruler who leads a tolerance-free theocracy is not suitable to be held for a center dealing with religious dialogue. Therefore, the center for us with this name, with this presentation, with this sponsor, is not acceptable with this principal payer. But we are already a step further, because the decision to set up the Centre in principle has been taken. Now it is about recognizing the status of an international organization. There are a lot of international institutions, which is not the first. However, the Centre is equipped with all the possible privileges that an international organization can have. This is in principle problematic, not just because of the centre, but because we have to consider whether these entire privileges are acceptable, appropriate, equal to the 21st century. ==References== If I have a little in this agreement between the Republic and the International King Abdullah, Abdulaziz is the center for interreligious and intercultural dialogue, there is already in Article 5 the exemption from jurisdiction and other measures: ‘The Centre shall be exempt from jurisdiction and enforcement with the exception of the following cases’, such as insignificant cases. That is, Austria gives up its rights for the center, its building and, essentially, all its employees. This goes so far as to prevent us from taking administrative action there as well as other international organisations and embassies. We cannot even enforce the construction order or the monument protection. If the leadership of this centre came to the idea of tearing off, remodeling, remodeling, remodeling, the listed palace in which it is located, we could do nothing at all – an insanity! That's how it goes. Let us perhaps go to Article 10, the exemption from taxes and duties: ‘The Centre and its property shall be exempt from all forms of taxation.’ This even concerns indirect taxes on things that are consumed. In other words, if representatives of the Centre buy something subject to VAT, the International Centre has a right to be recovered. Is that true? Is that acceptable? Is this acceptable in the sense of equal treatment for citizens? [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] I don't think so. It can't be! It also surprises me that there is no protest from the Social Democratic side, where there is always talk about the drying of tax islands, about the fight against tax relief possibilities, about the elimination of privileges and tax havens and the like. But here new ones are created – not only for the centre, I would like to clarify this once again, but for many equally organized organisations. Social security: It is even so far that Article 12 sets out the following: ‘The Centre and its staff shall be exempt from all compulsory contributions to the social security institutions of the Republic of Austria.’ I can only say that can't be true! These are people who are living here who need to be provided medically, socially and so on, and yet they are exempt from all compulsory contributions. They can insure themselves voluntarily everywhere, then the health insurance fund is responsible, but they are exempt from compulsory contributions, not only the bosses, the board of directors, but all employees of the center. Also interesting is article 14: Staff are exempt from the taxation of salaries, payments including allowances, remunerations, compensations, retirement and so on. They shall also be exempt from all forms of taxation of income which they or their employees or their relatives obtain in the common household from sources outside the Republic of Austria. This means a total and general exemption from all taxes and charges for everyone working there. What are the factual reasons for this in 2013? - None at all. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] The only reason is that it is a historical old load that has also been granted to others. This goes far back, far ahead of the 1953 Vienna agreement, in which this is in principle so sketched. However, the privileges are maintained critically and repeatedly acknowledged. This goes so far that even the employees' motor vehicles – not constantly, but every four years – are free of value added tax and by the way – what is the name of the tax on new weights? [[Call the FPÖ:NoVA!]] – NoVA can be purchased free of charge. This is only possible every four years, so employees cannot buy a car every year and then sell it on, but every four years they can introduce a car to Austria free of VAT and VAT. That is completely incomprehensible, completely crazy! And that's how it goes. I've just picked out the highlights. I believe that there is a need for a fundamental debate here, especially in view of the fact that the King Abdullah Center has also been granted a most favourable clause, which means nothing more than that, if any other organization is granted a right beyond that granted to the Abdullah Centre, this right should be automatically granted to the Abdullah Centre. Honestly, when I look into the round, is that fair and fair? Is this legislation and decision-making of the year 2013 in the democratic Republic of Austria? – In my opinion, quite clearly: No, no, no, Thank you.

It is true that we have dealt with this centre several times both in the Committee on Foreign Affairs and here in plenary. Today, the government proposal for the office agreement is a debate. As mentioned in the Committee, I have made the effort to compare this Headquarters Agreement with similar Headquarters Agreements, which also apply to international organisations based in Austria. In comparison, I noticed something very exciting, namely that these agreements are identical in many respects, but not on a very important point, namely on privileges and immunities. While it is common practice among other organisations that the Headquarters Agreement states that the organisation naturally undertakes to stop its employees from misusing their privileges, the Saudi Centre speaks of the Centre as ‘provisioning’ this. Now I would not have become so skeptical if it were another centre, a less controversial one, one where Saudi Wahhabism is not represented as a so-called world religion, not one where the representative of wah-habitian Islam can also determine advisers in the hundred-member advisory council of this center. All of this has been massive criticisms, and they remain upright, as you all know. I have also found it interesting that the Foreign Minister, on my question, has not actually known a substantive answer with the privileges and immunities in this agreement, which is much more mildly formulated. Interestingly, he has said that he will submit it in writing, but that has not happened until today. However, the office agreement in the Committee on Foreign Affairs was rejected, and today it is to be finally decided. This is a process that makes, mildly said, very bold and makes even more suspicions happen; for it cannot be that in such a controversial project, where all of us are not yet aware of how the outcome will be, the immunities and the privileges are very lax and the centre is not stoned and clearly committed to instructing the employees not to misuse these immunities, but only to a mild prospect. A similar, comparable office agreement even mentions that the Director-General shall take all measures to ensure that the privileges and immunities granted under this Agreement are not abused and so on and so on. Nor is such a provision mentioned in the Saudi Office Agreement. In summary, there are several reasons here to reject this Headquarters Agreement. There have even been additional reasons to strengthen our suspicion. That is why we will not vote in favour of this office agreement. We will, of course, continue to monitor and monitor the work of this centre. follow. The responsibility, if something goes wrong there, which is our fear, is borne by the government groups and by those political groups that have taken part. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e13552	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! High house! The legal complaint is in fact the conclusion of a whole package of motions for resolutions submitted on the occasion of the introduction of the national administration courts and the Federal Administrative Court. We are therefore fulfilling the final point of our agreements. I would therefore like to thank all my colleagues in the Constitutional Committee for their excellent climate, which has led us all to work out our points and to get all the points done. – Thank you very much for this cooperation! But I am particularly grateful to the employees who have not always had it easy to find a solution with our very difficult ways. I really can say thank you to all the employees who have been there. [[Congratulation with SPÖ and ÖVP as well as the Abg. Dr. Fichtenbauer.]] I am particularly pleased that we have found a unanimous solution to a rather difficult and controversial debate, and I would like to highlight the history of this law. We had decided on a motion for a resolution on the occasion of the administrative reform of the law that we would allow a legislative complaint, namely that the parties should finally have the opportunity to challenge a law that is based on a decision, if they believe it is unconstitutional. That was once the fundamental motion for a resolution. Then we got a bill from the Constitutional Service. There has already been a very controversial debate: on the one hand, ordinary jurisdiction, on the other hand constitutional jurisdiction. It was too close to us, even as the political leaders, to the judgment that we all did not want to introduce. I do not want to hide the fact that the breakthrough has actually come on the basis of a presentation by Mr Fichtenbauer, who has proposed an interim procedure. We then tried to bring this interim procedure into a legislative form. I think we have really succeeded in finding a solution in many, many discussions with all those who are both an equivalent to the Constitutional Court and also an equidistance to the Supreme Court, which represented two very contrary and extreme positions, thus making the golden means the basis of decision. What does that mean? – We have granted the possibility that, in an ordinary appeal against a decision at first instance, the parties are given the opportunity to make a complaint or to bring an appeal with the appeal or separately from it. This means an essential and substantial added value for the legal protection of individual citizens, an added value in the range of our legal protection opportunities, and I think this is a very, very reasonable solution that we have found. There are two concerns that need to be taken into account. One is that there should be no delays, no infinite delays in the procedure. And there are certain procedures which are not suitable for such a legal complaint. This is the insolvency procedure and, above all, the execution procedure, because decisions are taken that are no longer reversible. It is therefore justified in this area, too, if there are exceptions. A second topic, which actually appeared only at the end of the discussions, was the area of public books. To ensure that confidence in the public books is not weakened, but is still guaranteed, one assumes that, if a legislative complaint occurs in this process, the procedure must either be interrupted or it will not be eligible for entry in this area. The implementation of these exceptions has been transferred to the simple legislator, and we have pointed out in detail in our explanations and explanations that this must not lead to the weakening of the legal complaint, but may only be in special cases, and therefore we have excluded these legal instruments. Again: I think that's a sensible way we've gone. I think it's an increase in legal protection. I believe that at the end of our package of state administration, we have really brought together something. And thanks again to everyone, because it was a parade example of how to reach an excellent solution in Parliament itself through parliamentary discussion.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e13623	The next item is the speech by Mr Magistrate. Gerstl. 4 minutes of voluntary time limit. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e13644	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to start by thanking my previous speaker, all the speakers in the constitutional area, all the officials of the Federal Chancellor's Office, the Vice-Cancellor's Office, to all those who have contributed – I think we really cannot thank enough here. But I would like to make my remarks a little more general. The details from the final negotiations have already explained exactly what my previous speaker was talking about. It is important for me to point out to the public, above all, what it is all about and what great steps we have taken. In fact, with today's decision, we are putting the last stone under a whole series of major constitutional reforms and administrative reforms, which we are discussing during this legislative period. have concluded – most of them also have unanimously or almost unanimously. This really needs recognition and respect. I therefore believe that this House can also be proud of itself, of these reforms, which it has decided here and will now also decide on today. This legal complaint, which we discussed for the first time in 2004 at the Austrian Convention, and where there has actually been a large majority in favour of doing so, is it really what it is about? – It is, quite simply, for people in our country that no one can be condemned or judged in civil proceedings under a constitutional law. It is actually a matter of course that no one can be condemned or judged for a constitutional law. But, good thing just needs a while, and it took up to the present day that this is really the case now. This really makes us a milestone in the rule of law. Our rule of law system is, I believe, on such a high standard that many countries in the world envy us about it. This is true, I believe, also here, of the fact that such legal certainty is not only crucial for our citizens here, but also for all investors who come to Austria and who can then rely on such a rule of law – as they cannot find in other countries. However, with this legal complaint, we are also taking a step towards giving people more freedom and more responsibility. They are no longer so dependent on the institutions, and they are now, in particular, no longer dependent on the fact that they have to ask a judge to refer this to the Constitutional Court for them in the event of a possible constitutional law, but any party that would be affected by it itself can now and should even come to the Constitutional Court. This is also an expression of how we deal with our citizens or, in the legal sense, how to deal with our legal subjects. So we can now go away from this expression, there are no more subjects, but these are equal citizens who can now exercise their own right and no longer have to take action. I think this is a very big step we have taken here. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dr. Fichtenbauer.]] At the same time, in the many discussions we conducted last year, we have succeeded in removing the two major criticisms that came after the review of our first draft or the draft Constitutional Service, which we sent last summer. The two major criticisms were procedural delay on the one hand and on the other hand a change in the equality of the highest courts. Both points are not only depleted, but they are actually cleared now. With the interim procedure already carried out by Mr Wittmann, and with the special support of Mr Fichtenbauer, we have succeeded in effectively excluding this delay in the ordinary appeal process. I'll save you the details now. It is, I believe, not necessary to carry it out here, but it is simply worth noting that there can no longer be any delay in proceedings here. Furthermore, with the situation that a complaint to the Constitutional Court is already possible in the appeal proceedings, it is also excluded that a so-called appeal is brought, which would also be accompanied by a referral from the Constitutional Court to the Supreme Court. This is also excluded, and the Constitutional Court actually becomes as active as the was already foreseen in the Austrian federal constitution of the 20th century, namely as a negative legislator in the control of standards. In this respect, this is not a system failure, but a special system development that will lead to greater legal protection for citizens. Ladies and gentlemen! Let me conclude by thanking once again all those who have contributed here. Sometimes it's like Max Weber said: Politics is drilling hard boards! This has shown itself very clearly in this field, but it is also necessary to put up with patience in order to reach with passion the goal that is needed: to improve the legal protection for citizens for an even better rule of law, as it is exemplary for Europe and the whole world. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e14321	Thank you, Madam President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! I try to explain briefly what we decide here, because I feel it is so technical and it has been discussed so much in detail that many have not been able to follow exactly what this development is and why so many positive words are found here. It is about the possibility that a law that is unconstitutional may be applied in court proceedings. So far, it has been the case that a party has been able to encourage the Constitutional Court to consider whether this law is unconstitutional. You could only stimulate this, and you're always saying, I'm not entitled to it, so I don't know if that's going to be accepted or not. – The court then decided on its own initiative: Let me present this to or not to the Constitutional Court. That is the current state, and that was unsatisfactory, because then a party may have been given a judgment in which a law that was unconstitutional was applied. On the other hand, our initiative – i.e. the initiative of Dr. Fichtenbauer and of me – has turned, by saying that the whole thing should become a right, a right of the party. And that's what we're doing here now. In other words, in the future, the party can hold in court proceedings, as it has just been said: I am of the opinion that the law that was applied on the basis of which my judgment was given is unconstitutional. I am of this opinion and I therefore demand that this be examined by the Constitutional Court. - That's exactly what we decide now. This has been a real breach of law, because it has been possible until now that a party has not come to its right if the court has not submitted it. That is why these many positive words, and that is why this joy, not least in the Group of the Party of European Socialists, has been implemented. [[Congratulations with the FPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] Two, three points. One essential point was the delay in the proceedings. It was argued here that a problem would be that any defender or any lawyer would, of course, make an immediate request to do so and thus delay any procedure. – We took this into account. It is now true that, on the one hand, it is not at all possible to make such a request in certain cases – where it is not appropriate – and on the other hand, the Constitutional Court has clearly promised to take a decision within four months, which corresponds to the period between the first and the second or, in general, between two instances, until further proceedings are taken. So there is no delay here. We have also taken account of the problem of a so-called judgment complaint – that is, that a judgment by a judicial institution, by a higher court, the Constitutional Court could be examined and repealed – and that is not the case here. . So we have cleared out the two essential points, and I therefore believe that this is a very successful reform. Why the whole thing came about was speculated here. Perhaps, because the government was not involved – an essential point – but I believe that a special success recipe was that Dr. Fichtenbauer put together expertise and persistence here and pushed the whole thing forward, that is clearly to be said. And then there has also been, as has already been mentioned, the very constructive climate in itself in the Constitutional Committee, for which I should also like to thank the Committee president, Dr Wittmann, who does so regularly. [[Consistency with FPÖ and SPÖ as well as with the Abg. Disc]] In this sense: This is a very encouraging conclusion to a legal gap, and I am pleased that we can decide together.

For me, it is incomprehensible that human dignity is not in the Austrian constitution. In Germany, it is in Article 1 of the Constitution. Human dignity is not a philosophical concept, even the newspaper “Die Zeit” now has a great focus on this subject, but it is a basis for human rights and the basis for all rights of protection and freedom. That is why I would like us to anchor the human dignity in Article 7 of the Federal Constitution and hope that we will find an agreement here in the Constitutional Committee before the summer. Thank you.

I would like to highlight the question: What does this administrative jurisdiction bring to citizens? – On the one hand, a reduction in the removal of the instance is due to the fact that existing authorities and therefore, in many cases, a reduction in the transfer of the instance is necessary. A clear and efficient government structure also means that the overall process is becoming cheaper and the decision-making period is shorter, thereby reducing the overall procedure. I also think that it will bring about an increase in the quality of decisions, because the administrative courts are the result of a concentration of all appeal procedures and, in my view, centres of competence. However, there is also an increased proximity to citizens, because it is simply a simplification with these judicial authorities, with the competence of these judicial authorities as a whole. In my opinion, it is also important for legal protection seekers to have a uniform and clearly structured address for administrative courts in the future. Ladies and gentlemen! In my opinion, the implementation of the administrative jurisdiction is a very large reform approach, and I am pleased if I am on 1st. This administrative jurisdiction will enter into force on January 2014. Thank you.

I presented an overview in the Constitutional Committee: 131 individual laws have now been passed. 161 have already been decided in the government and will hopefully be decided either today or in the July plenary session of the National Council. The Presidents and Vice-Presidents of both the Federal Administrative Court of First Instance and the Federal Finance Court were appointed last year so that the relevant preparatory work can be carried out so that this large administrative reform project can actually be implemented. The takeover procedures for those judges who have a right to take over have been completed. The invitation to tender for further judicial and judicial positions has been made. The assessments are done. The hearings are in progress right now. And the decision to order these judges is scheduled in July. The search for candidates for additional legal staff and non-academic staff will be launched in the next few days. The reference of the building in Vienna, a building rented by the BIG for this then largest court of Austria, is scheduled to take place in October and November, and then the whole with 1. In January 2014, operational operations were started. I can't tell you if everything works. After all the efforts that have been and will be made, I assume that it will work. So all advance measures are on schedule as we have done. The Presidents, Vice-Presidents, the President and the Vice-President have begun their work on organisational implementation at the highest pace and are on schedule. And then I hope that what we all have done will actually become reality, namely that this will lead to faster procedures, to more rule of law, to more rule of law and to more uniform procedures. I assume that this is the case, that this goal, on which all of us have worked very intensively, will also be achieved. I would like to take this opportunity, because it is probably the last one in connection with the creation of the administrative jurisdiction, to thank everyone: at the Constitutional Service, which had to bear the great burden of legal activity, among the staff in the cabinets, namely at the Federal Chancellor's Office, Alexander Klingenbrunn, who is here, at the Vice-Canceller, Patrick Segalla, who, I believe, is here – I have seen him before – of course, with all the staff members of the clubs who have worked very intensively, that we have actually come to unanimous decisions, and of course also with you, with the constitutional speakers of the clubs who have worked very intensively on the subject. So thank you very much once again! Thank you, of course, also to those people who actually have to implement this, and all the good of those judges, employees, who then have to do so in the interests of the citizens of this country. Finally, as this is always used to, I would like to welcome a person sitting on the gallery: a young Carinthian student, Julia Schmid, politically interested – that is why she is here – and by the way she won the Vice-European Championship in the wild water canoe on Saturday. Congratulations! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e15964	Madam President. High house! In 1996, representatives of the FPÖ and the Greens initiated a popular animal welfare request with 496 000 signatures. In addition to the Animal Protection Act and the Animal Procuratorate, it was also required that animal welfare be included in the Constitution. The Animal Protection Act was then passed in 2004 and all the parties agreed that animal welfare should come into the Constitution as a state target. The negotiations have taken a long time, but it is now clear: “§ 2. The Republic of Austria (Bund, countries and municipalities) is committed to animal welfare.’ The explanations that are as important to the interpretation of a law as the law itself contain: "This is in line with the June 2004 resolution, and the aim of animal welfare as a state target in the Constitution, in order to take into account the command of a morally responsible human dealings with the animal as a feeling being. Further provisions are not necessary, as in § 1 of the Animal Protection Act, BGBl. I No. 118/2004, already set as an objective to protect the life and well-being of animals from the special responsibility of humans for the animal as a fellow animal. In addition, § 285a ABGB points out that animals are not things and are protected by special laws.” The allegation that Paragraphs 5 and 6 of this Regulation repeals these provisions disproves the explanations as follows: “The production of high-quality food in Austria, as well as the sustainable exploitation of natural resources, is a central concern in the sense of the Austrian population and its security of supply and should therefore be taken into account accordingly within the framework of the state objectives.” – So again: within the framework of § 2. And also to § 6, research, it says: “The purpose of this provision is to highlight the importance of research with regard to the other national objectives.” – In view of the other state objectives. So, ladies and gentlemen, my request to anchor animal welfare as a state target in the Constitution is fulfilled. I would like to thank, on the one hand, the numerous animal welfare organisations which have fought for years to ensure that animal welfare comes into the Constitution, first of all the VGT and four Pfoten. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Brunner]] I would like to thank the animal protection speakers for the fact that we have fought together for many years, with Mr Keck von der SPÖ, Mr Brunner from the Greens and Mr Spadiut from the BZÖ. I would like to thank Mr Graf, who has been working in the Bureau to ensure that this has always come on the agenda. I would particularly like to thank the three colleagues in the Constitutional Committee, Mr Wittmann, Mr Gerstl and Mr Fichtenbauer, who have finally found a joint formulation. Of course, one can demand more and more, one can wish for more, a compromise is always a compromise. [[Abg. Pendl: But a good one!]] This is a common way for the High House where we can go. It's a good compromise. That is why I can conclude by saying: This is a beautiful day for the animals, a special day for animal protection in Austria!

ParlaMint-AT_2013-06-13-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00207_d8e16030	Madam President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! State objective – I will take a little further away from the discussion that will now take place here from the speaker's desk –: A state objective is something other than a simple legal implementation of various regulations that can be made in detail. [[Abg. May. Brunner: Exactly!]] In the Committee, then, words have been like making a grievable state target. – The Constitution has no eligible provisions. The Constitution contains overarching rules from which simple legal provisions are derived, which can then be sued. That is to say, something is going by, Mrs Colleague, if we are to say that we have to include a quantifiable provision in the Constitution. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Brunner.]] This is impossible according to our constitution, because it does not provide for it! That's why you have to say, okay, the simple legal provision continues to do so – and that's what it does in animal welfare. I am referring in particular to animal welfare, which is a full commitment to animal welfare! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] [[Bravoruf des Abg. Pendl.]] They have also claimed that there is an over- and subordination of state targets here, but that is not the case in our Constitution! There are no overarching state objectives and no subordinate national targets. The objectives of the State are equally close to each other and should they be contradicted, the Constitutional Court has the task of assessing a simple legal provision in accordance with these constitutional principles, whether it has been constitutionally established, is acceptable in terms of content or not. And that is why there is no subordination under research and not overordination of research, not subordination among sustainable agriculture, but an equal state target, which is as much or less valuable as the other national objectives. The simple legal version, the respective implementing law, is then indictable and also usable. Should that be too little, one can go to the Constitutional Court and say that the State objective does not find the appropriate precipitation, and then the Constitutional Court could intervene. But every law – every law! – is subject to this State objective. It is also subject to this State objective in its application and challenge, together with its explanations and interpretation by the Constitutional Court. In other words, full animal protection is parallel to research and others to which we confess ourselves. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Brunner.]] It is in the weighting of the individual law how they ultimately work together. I know this discussion of other national objectives. We have also had this with children's rights, and this works very well with the elaboration of children's rights. At that time, 24 pages, namely the UN resolution, were to be written into the Constitution. Ladies and gentlemen! The Constitution is not a reading book! The Constitution has principles to formulate – and that does it!

This means that citizens of the European Union, the European Economic Area and Switzerland, who pursue self-employed or self-employed employment in the federal territory, and whose families also live with us, enjoy the Austrian family aid is all right. However, it is not possible for the families who do not live in Austria and whose fathers or mothers work here, the cost of living will not be adjusted to the respective recipient country. For a child in Austria you get 130,90 € children's allowance per month. In comparison, the family allowance in Slovakia – the cost of living there is 35 percent lower than ours – is € 17.81 per month. This means that we deliver more than €100 to this country without this service being equal to the actual cost of living. We would find it fairer if this family aid were to be adapted to the cost of living in the beneficiary country concerned. With regard to any concerns that might arise in legal terms, constitutional and European law experts have considered that such a design would be in the sense of a non-discrimination regime and that a reduction in family aid would be legal. We're helping save here. If we really want to put FLAF on the front and save money, it would be a way to make savings.

In any event, the proposed scheme is EU-consistency, as the EU Treaty lays down in Article 12 a prohibition of discrimination which does not allow for a restriction on the free movement of persons. The Social Security Co-ordination Regulation states in two articles – Articles 7 and 67 – that children should not be made worse than under national law, just because they live in another Member State. Both the Commission and the European Court of Justice have a very strict legal interpretation in this regard. Apart from the fact that the application contradicts EU law, this would entail a significant administrative burden, because if you count everything together, you would have to calculate your own performance levels for a total of 31 countries. Of course, these would have to be managed accordingly. In any case, my group will not agree to your request. In Austria, we have framework conditions for families, which consist of a balanced mix of cash and benefits in kind, which can be seen in the EU comparison and even in the international comparison. Federal Minister Mitterlehner is also committed to further improvements in family benefits. He is concerned with increasing family aid. It is about simplifying family aid.
